<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Issue date 2025 Jul 15.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<p>This is an open access article under the CC BY-NC license (<a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/</a>).</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC12991868  NIHMSID: NIHMS2140314  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40381893/" class="usa-link">40381893</a>
</div>
<div class="ra xbox p" role="complementary" aria-label="Related or updated information about this article"><div>The publisher's version of this article is available at <a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuroimage.2025.121248" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Neuroimage</a>
</div></div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="ABS1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p id="P1">Detecting cognitive states and impairments through EEG signals is crucial for applications in aviation and medicine and has broad applications in the field of human–machine interaction. However, existing methods often fail to capture the fine-grained neural dynamics of critical brain processes due to limited temporal resolution and inadequate signal decomposition techniques. To address this, we introduce the Spectral Intensity Stability (SIS) algorithm, a novel technique that analyzes the stability and competition of dominant brain frequency oscillations across granular timescales (≈4 ms). Unlike traditional spectral methods, SIS captures rapid neural transitions and hierarchical frequency dynamics, enabling more accurate characterization of task-specific cognitive processes. Our study focuses on EEG data from pilots performing multitasking simulations under hypoxic and non-hypoxic conditions, a high-stakes scenario where cognitive performance is crucial. We divided this multitasking scenario into specific cognitive states, such as task precursor, interruption, execution, and recovery. Our algorithm SIS achieved a 29.8% improvement in cognitive state classification compared to conventional methods, demonstrating superior accuracy in distinguishing both task states and hypoxic impairments. This work is novel because it bridges gaps left by traditional methods by revealing the role of hierarchical spectral dynamics in maintaining cognitive performance. Through the Granular Analysis Informing Neural Stability (GAINS) framework, we reveal how neuronal groups self-organize across fine-grained time scales, providing new understanding of task-switching, neural communication, and criticality. The findings highlight the potential for developing real-time cognitive monitoring systems to enhance safety and performance in environments where cognitive impairments can have serious consequences. Future research should extend these insights by incorporating transient behaviors and spatial dynamics to achieve a more comprehensive framework for characterizing cognitive states.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Electroencephalography (EEG), Entropy, Neural oscillations, Neural criticality, Task prediction, Newell’s time scale, Cognitive processes, Spectral stability</p></section></section><section id="S1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p id="P2">Electroencephalogram (EEG) signals capture information about electrical activity in the human brain, which has been widely used to detect and classify cognitive states and cognitive impairments. However, current state detection methods often overlook the rich information contained within oscillatory brain activity (neural oscillations) due to limitations in existing signal processing tools and techniques. Neural oscillations, categorized as delta (0–3.5 Hz), theta (4–7 Hz), alpha (8–12 Hz), beta (13–40 Hz), and gamma (32–100 Hz) bands, encode information about the synchronization of neural firing, which facilitates communication within and between brain regions. While this communication and its relationship to cognitive states are well understood, the underlying neural mechanisms driving cognitive functionality remain unclear (<a href="#R9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R9">Castro-Sanchez et al., 2018</a>; <a href="#R11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R11">Chialvo, 2010</a>).</p>
<p id="P3">Capturing and characterizing these underlying mechanisms and processes could enable the development of more advanced cognitive state detection systems, ultimately enhancing human–machine interaction. Therefore, it is imperative to improve signal processing tools and techniques to better analyze and decode the dynamics and neural mechanisms underlying cognition.</p>
<section id="S2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">1.1. Prior work</h3>
<p id="P4">To better understand the dynamics underlying cognition and cognitive processes, it is essential to develop methods for extracting information from human brain activity, such as EEG signals. This study explores the underlying dynamics of how multiple neural oscillatory groups organize to fire together, leading to the emergence of new groups or the inhibition of competing neural oscillations. These organizational transitions can be described as neural criticality, a phenomenon where a system undergoes a phase transition (<a href="#R34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R34">Rocha et al., 2022</a>; <a href="#R15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R15">Hesse and Gross, 2014</a>; <a href="#R3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R3">Beggs, 2008</a>). While the detailed mechanisms of neural criticality and brain activity dynamics remain only partially understood, previous research suggests that healthy brain activity operates near critical phase transitions. These boundaries, often referred to as critical points, delineate synchronized and asynchronous neural activity (<a href="#R34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R34">Rocha et al., 2022</a>; <a href="#R11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R11">Chialvo, 2010</a>; <a href="#R22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R22">Massobrio et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R47">Vidiella et al., 2021</a>).</p>
<p id="P5">We hypothesize that these critical points are fundamental to the brain’s information-processing capabilities and that different cognitive tasks may rely on distinct types of organizational processes. These processes facilitate more effective communication between neural oscillator groups, giving rise to unique cognitive functions (<a href="#R34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R34">Rocha et al., 2022</a>; <a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Chen and Gong, 2019</a>; <a href="#R11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R11">Chialvo, 2010</a>; <a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Shew et al., 2015</a>). This raises a fundamental question: <em>If different types of organizational processes are conditioned for specific cognitive functions, how can we decode EEG signals to appropriately characterize these processes in a way that reflects their respective critical points?</em></p>
<section id="S3"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Human Activity &amp; Criticality Across Timescales:</h4>
<p id="P6">To begin addressing this fundamental research question, we draw from <a href="#R28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R28">Newell (1990)</a>, who discusses the various time spans across different time scales that define the range of human activities, from the biological to the social band. This framework provides the basis for a proper <em>granular analysis</em> necessary to uncover how neuronal groups organize and interact. Human activities can be characterized across time scales spanning 12 orders of magnitude, from processes occurring within organelles (i.e., the biological band; 10<sup>−4</sup> s) to activities within and across societies (i.e., the social band; 10<sup>7</sup> s) [see <a href="#T1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>].</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="T1"><h5 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h5>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="P7">According to Newell’s framework, cognitive behaviors often emerge from the novel integration of processes occurring at finer levels of analysis. For example, observable actions performed at the operations time scale (approximately 1 s, such as moving a mouse pointer and clicking on an object; see <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>) are driven by cognitive processes occurring at the deliberate acts time scale (approximately 100 ms). Within this hierarchical structure, processes and outcomes at shorter time scales serve as the foundation or inputs for more complex cognitive processes that unfold over longer durations (see <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F1"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 1.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a0fb/12991868/57c1c0047e27/nihms-2140314-f0001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="824" width="1794" alt="Fig. 1."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P87">A Goals, Operators, Methods, and Selection Rules (GOMS) model illustrating deliberate acts organized into the operation of moving a mouse pointer to a target and clicking on the target (adapted from <a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">Freed and Remington, 2000</a>). The numbers above each process indicate the process duration in milliseconds, while the lines between the processes represent dependencies. The duration of this operation ranges from 450 ms to 1152 ms.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P8">To enhance cognitive task detection, these finer-grained time scales – such as deliberate acts (10<sup>−1</sup> seconds) and operations (10<sup>0</sup> seconds) – should be examined as they contain critical information about the organizational dynamics of neuronal oscillatory groups, specifically the activation of neural criticality associated with underlying cognitive processes. However, based on the principles of criticality and Newell’s defined time bands, current methodologies are insufficient to effectively capture this level of temporal resolution and its dynamics. This creates a significant gap in our ability to decode neuronal firing patterns, cognitive processes, and impairments occurring within these critical granular time scales (100 ms to 1 s).</p>
<p id="P9">The concept of criticality, when viewed through the lens of Newell’s time scale, suggests a potential relationship between smaller time scales (i.e., building blocks) that exhibit criticality and contribute to the emergence of larger processes, such as cognition. This self-organization process involves neurons firing synchronously, triggering additional neurons to fire in a coordinated manner, thereby altering the state of the globally recorded neuronal network.</p>
<p id="P10">To advance our understanding of neuronal dynamics, it is crucial to develop analytical methods capable of decomposing signals into distinct neuronal oscillatory groups within granular time scales. Such granular analysis would enable a deeper exploration of the interactions and self-organization of these oscillatory groups, providing valuable insights into the mechanisms that underpin the stability of neuronal firing, criticality, and their roles in cognitive processes. Consequently, an enhanced <em>Granular Analysis Informing Neural Stability (GAINS)</em> framework could offer the necessary information to effectively discriminate between different cognitive processes.</p></section><section id="S4"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Current Analysis Limitations for State Detection:</h4>
<p id="P11">Traditional EEG signal decomposition methods, such as Fourier analysis, aim to extract a signal’s intensity at specific EEG frequency oscillations (bands) to obtain spectral features, which are then associated with cognitive behaviors, processes, learning, and impairments (<a href="#R46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R46">van Dongen-Boomsma et al., 2010</a>; <a href="#R2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R2">Beatty and Figueroa, 1974</a>; <a href="#R49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R49">Zhang et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#R18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R18">Jirsa and Müller, 2013</a>; <a href="#R36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R36">Rodriguez-Larios and Alaerts, 2019</a>). The Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) accomplishes this by projecting the time-domain EEG signal into the frequency domain, consisting of its associated frequency and phase components (see the central plot of <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a> for the frequency spectrum). However, in analyzing cognition using traditional Fourier approaches, EEG signals can only be resolved using window lengths on the order of seconds (e.g., one second (<a href="#R49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R49">Zhang et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#R36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R36">Rodriguez-Larios and Alaerts, 2019</a>), two seconds (<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Hsiao et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R46">van Dongen-Boomsma et al., 2010</a>), four seconds <a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">Schutter et al., 2006</a>; <a href="#R18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R18">Jirsa and Müller, 2013</a>). While this allows for blunt characterization of spectral intensity, it neglects information on spectral intensity fluctuations, rendering traditional approaches incapable of capturing events of criticality and the neural communication between oscillatory groups. <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a> illustrates this limitation by showing how traditional methods fail to capture events of criticality and changes within oscillatory groups.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F2"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 2.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a0fb/12991868/66ff025f6b8f/nihms-2140314-f0002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1516" width="1585" alt="Fig. 2."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P88">Potential EEG spectral intensity dynamics for characterizing cognitive task transitions are illustrated. These dynamics are broadly categorized into five states: Converging, Unstable, High Complexity, Diverging, and Stable. The arrows depict the possible transitions of these dynamics over time, emphasizing the relevance of Newell’s timescale in decoding cognitive processes. Stability can either increase (gray arrows) or decrease (black arrows). This framework introduces the concept of Spectral Intensity Stability (SIS), where different frequency bands exhibit distinct hierarchical arrangements and stable dynamics, potentially forming a cognitive process or state. Each graph represents a different level of SIS that could occur within a given time window.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P12">The temporal spectral intensity dynamics, as shown in the figures surrounding the central blue plot in <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>, can vary significantly over time. However, these dynamics become indistinguishable when the temporal dimension is collapsed, leaving only the frequency content in the central plot. This obscures critical points and dynamics necessary for distinguishing potential cognitive states, such as Unstable, Converging, High Disorder, Stable, and Diverging. Each of these states reflects distinct neuronal firing criticality and neural communication patterns, yet projects the same frequency domain representation. Consequently, traditional frequency spectrum analysis only measures the intensity of frequencies during a time window but fails to capture the dynamics of hierarchical arrangements or the stability of neuronal populations within that period. Periods with vastly different dynamics may produce identical Power Spectral Densities (PSD), providing no information about the hierarchical arrangement or stability of neural oscillatory groups.</p>
<p id="P13">This underscores the need to develop novel methods capable of accurately representing cognitive dynamics and their relationship to cognitive processing and states. Current analyses overlook critical temporal scales (such as those defined by Newell’s cognitive bands within unit operations and deliberate acts) and the nature of hierarchical arrangements of neuronal oscillatory groups within a single spatial location. Alternative approaches are required to fully capture the underlying mechanisms and dynamics of criticality associated with cognitive processes.</p>
<p id="P14">Existing metrics, such as the Engagement Index (EI) (<a href="#R31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R31">Pope et al., 1995</a>), have shown potential in measuring the hierarchical arrangement between neural oscillations and classifying cognitive states. However, these methods lack the granularity needed to characterize dynamics adequately and provide only blunt approximations (<a href="#R31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R31">Pope et al., 1995</a>; <a href="#R32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R32">Prinzel et al., 2000</a>; <a href="#R4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R4">Berka et al., 2007</a>). Therefore, the development of advanced metrics that quantify the dynamic hierarchical arrangements of neural oscillations within appropriate temporal scales (as defined by Newell’s cognitive bands) could offer significant insights into the criticality underlying a “fingerprint” for specific cognitive processes.</p>
<p id="P15">This leads to two fundamental research questions regarding criticality and cognitive processes:
</p>
<ul id="L1" class="list" style="list-style-type:none">
<li>
<span class="label">RQ1</span><p class="display-inline" id="P16">Do unique dynamics within these task events that elicit criticality have unique dynamics, potentially establishing a “fingerprint” for cognitive processes?</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">RQ2</span><p class="display-inline" id="P17">Does the depression of neuronal firing due to hypoxia lead to events of criticality with unique spectral arrangements?</p>
</li>
</ul></section></section><section id="S5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">1.2. Challenges</h3>
<p id="P18">Methods capable of extracting both granular spectral and temporal information are essential to effectively capture critical points and transitions in neural activation within cognitive states. Traditional time–frequency decomposition methods, such as the Short-Time Fourier Transform (STFT), suffer from an inherent tradeoff between time and frequency resolution (<a href="#R42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R42">Spyers-Ashby et al., 1998</a>; <a href="#R19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R19">Kıymık et al., 2005</a>; <a href="#R50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R50">Zhang et al., 2023</a>). Because STFT uses a fixed window size across all frequencies, it struggles to resolve both high-frequency transients and low-frequency rhythms simultaneously. This limitation makes it difficult to detect rapid changes in neural dynamics that are important for decoding cognitive state transitions. Wavelet analysis has emerged as a promising alternative to Fourier-based methods for identifying instances of criticality (<a href="#R17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R17">Huang et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">Bohbot et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R43">Ter Wal et al., 2020</a>). Wavelets offer improved temporal granularity by using scalable basis functions that create filter banks with center frequencies and bandwidths arranged linearly or logarithmically (<a href="#R20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R20">Kosciessa et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#R43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R43">Ter Wal et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#R33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R33">Purcell et al., 2017</a>). However, conventional wavelet transforms also face resolution limitations. Wavelet filters are non-orthogonal and overlapping in the frequency domain, which results in redundant frequency components and imprecise signal reconstruction. This makes it difficult to isolate specific neuronal oscillations such as delta, theta, alpha, beta, and gamma (<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Napoli et al., 2020</a>). Overlapping wavelets can distort the representation of intensity across frequency bands. This may lead to the overestimation or underestimation of neural activity, reducing the interpretability of the results. Thus, current approaches such as STFT and wavelet-based methods fall short in capturing the complex, transient, and hierarchical dynamics of brain activity.</p>
<p id="P19">To quantify the hierarchical arrangement and irregularity (stability) between frequency oscillations, researchers have turned to entropic methods, particularly spectral entropy (<a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">Nunes et al., 2004</a>). Spectral entropy, derived from Shannon entropy (<a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">Nunes et al., 2004</a>; <a href="#R45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R45">Tian et al., 2017</a>), uses the spectral intensity of each frequency oscillation within a power spectrum of brain activity as probabilities for entropy calculation. A high spectral entropy value indicates a uniform distribution of spectral intensity across frequencies, while a low value reflects a power spectrum concentrated within a singular frequency bin (<a href="#R1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R1">Abásolo et al., 2006</a>). Although spectral entropy has been used to explore the mechanistic characteristics of neural activation and firing through dominant frequency bands, it fails to capture irregularities and hierarchical arrangements in frequency band activity.</p>
<p id="P20">To address temporal irregularity, studies have employed other entropic methods, such as sample entropy (<a href="#R8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R8">Bruce et al., 2009</a>). While sample entropy captures temporal irregularity, it forfeits the ability to characterize the spectral features of hierarchical arrangements between neural oscillations. This disconnect creates a gap in understanding transitional neural activation associated with criticality, as current entropic methods fail to simultaneously capture both the spectral and temporal arrangements of neural oscillations. Consequently, the hierarchical arrangements and stability of neural communication, along with its underlying processes (e.g., points of criticality), remain unexplored. There is currently no metric capable of fully capturing the interactions of neural oscillations and how they arrange over time, leaving critical dynamics of neural activation and cognitive processes insufficiently understood.</p></section><section id="S6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">1.3. Insights</h3>
<p id="P21">As previously discussed, classical wavelet filter banks lack the spectral resolution necessary for accurate one-to-one intensity analysis of EEG band activity, resulting in redundant spectral information across multiple wavelet scales. Traditional wavelet methods, such as the continuous wavelet transform, are primarily designed to localize frequency content and transient behaviors within a signal. However, they do not inherently provide the direct intensity of the signal, which is critical for the interpretability of EEG data. This limitation hinders the ability to statistically compare the intensity of EEG frequency bands (neural oscillatory groups).</p>
<p id="P22">Optimized filter bank designs leveraging wavelet concepts have been developed to overcome these limitations, enabling one-to-one representation of intensity for EEG frequency oscillations (i.e., delta, theta, alpha, beta, and gamma) over time (<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Napoli et al., 2020</a>). This unique design allows for precise temporal analysis of the dynamics, intensity, and interactions of neuronal oscillations. These interactions may reflect synchronized neural activity, facilitating communication between oscillatory groups essential for cognitive processes. By analyzing critical points during cognitive processes, such filter banks could reveal enriched neural information vital for understanding cognitive mechanisms, task detection, and impaired brain function during cognitive tasks.</p>
<p id="P23">To investigate the mechanisms underlying neural criticality and communication, we utilize a hypoxia dataset in which participants perform cognitive tasks. Hypoxia, a condition characterized by insufficient oxygen supply at the tissue level, disrupts neural communication and firing due to ATP depletion (<a href="#R30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R30">Papadelis et al., 2007</a>; <a href="#R38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R38">Schneider and Strüder, 2009</a>; <a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Napoli et al., 2020</a>). This disruption impairs brain function during cognitive tasks. Examining the effects of hypoxia on neural communication and criticality activation provides valuable insights into the dynamics of neural communication and its relationship to cognitive states and impairments.</p>
<p id="P24">While spectral intensity dynamics offer significant insights into cognitive states (<a href="#R45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R45">Tian et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R35">Rodríguez-González et al., 2024</a>), current metrics are limited to static snapshots of the frequency spectrum (as illustrated in the central plot of <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>). This static approach overlooks the unique signatures of neural communication between oscillatory groups during criticality events, which may vary depending on the specific cognitive process or state. These variations are reflected in the shifting dominance of neural oscillations over time, as depicted in the surrounding plots of <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>. Capturing the temporal hierarchical arrangement of these oscillations is crucial for characterizing neural communication patterns and understanding transitions of criticality during cognitive processes.</p></section></section><section id="S7"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Methods: Data collection and breakdown of cognitive tasks</h2>
<section id="S8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Hypoxia dataset</h3>
<p id="P25">The study at NASA Langley Research Center (LaRC) investigated the impact of mild hypoxia on cognitive performance in aircraft pilots. With approval from the Institutional Review Board, 49 participants (41 males, 8 females; average age 43.3 years) gave informed consent. Before hypoxia was induced, all participants underwent screening for neurological and cardiovascular conditions. Each participant also had current hypoxia training certifications for normobaric hypoxia and some flight experience. Simulated altitudes of sea level (21% O<sub>2</sub>) and 15,000 ft (11.2% O<sub>2</sub>) were induced using an Environics, Inc. Reduced Oxygen Breathing Device (ROBD-2). During both conditions, participants completed 10 min sessions of the Multi-Attribute Task Battery (MATB-II) (<a href="#R37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R37">Santiago-Espada et al., 2011</a>) to assess cognitive performance. EEG data were collected using a 16-lead system, selected to minimize interference from the ROBD-2 oxygen mask (see <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>). Since participants were required to breathe through the mask, its backstrap could obstruct certain electrode placements. To ensure high-quality recordings while accommodating the mask, we positioned electrodes to avoid interference while maintaining as close an alignment as possible with the standard 10–20 system.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F3"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a0fb/12991868/ad94c712ec95/nihms-2140314-f0003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1629" width="1507" alt="Fig. 3."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P89">A visual representation of the EEG electrode placements used in this study, highlighted in green.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="S9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Explanation of MATB-II operation</h3>
<p id="P26">The NASA MATB-II (<a href="#R37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R37">Santiago-Espada et al., 2011</a>) presents a single interface (<a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>) with four task components: Tracking, Communications, Resource Management, and Systems Monitoring. For this study, analysis focused on the Tracking, Communications, and Resource Management tasks. All data were collected during the manual tracking mode, excluding the automatic tracking option. In the tracking task, participants used a joystick to keep a crosshair centered within a square on the display (<a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>). The communications task simulated radio communication, requiring participants to listen for their assigned call sign. If the call sign was mentioned, they adjusted the specified radio and frequency channel. If not, participants ignored the audio and maintained the current settings. Adjustments were made using either a mouse or a keyboard.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F4"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a0fb/12991868/1ac322834d69/nihms-2140314-f0004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1207" width="1613" alt="Fig. 4."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P90">The NASA MATB-II layout utilized in this study. Our analysis focuses exclusively on responses related to the Tracking, Communications, and Resource Management tasks.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="S10"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. MATB output and ground truth assignment</h3>
<p id="P27">The raw MATB output files were analyzed line by line to extract action statements and timestamps, which were then used to define the endpoints of distinct cognitive task states. This approach is novel, as MATB files have not previously been examined in this way to systematically define cognitive task states. By structuring MATB output into distinct cognitive phases, we introduce an innovative method for extracting insights into multitasking behavior and neural activity.</p>
<p id="P28">For the communication task, system-generated remarks were used to align key actions – such as task initiation, user responses, and task completion – with specific cognitive states, as shown in <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>. Based on these remarks, we identified five distinct cognitive states:</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F5"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a0fb/12991868/869d9e61848d/nihms-2140314-f0005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="503" width="1690" alt="Fig. 5."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P91">This figure provides a breakdown of the variables derived from the deconstruction of a MATB task. The variables <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M154" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M155" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>A</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M156" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>E</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M157" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>R</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M158" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>O</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> correspond to defined task states, representing different time windows within a task: task precursor, audio task interruption, task execution, task recovery, and other task, respectively. The events or actions that define the endpoints of these states are depicted along the <em>x</em>-axis of the timeline.</p></figcaption></figure><ul id="L2" class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p id="P29"><strong>Task Precursor</strong> (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M18" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>) — The period before the communication task begins. This state captures early neural interactions before the introduction of a secondary task, setting the stage for multitasking.</p></li>
<li><p id="P30"><strong>Audio Task Interruption</strong> (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M19" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>A</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>) — The moment when the participant first hears and responds to an audio cue, marking the onset of multitasking. It specifically occurs when the participant’s assigned call sign is mentioned, requiring them to adjust the radio frequency. This state reflects synchronized neural activity and auditory processing, including the participant’s auditory brainstem response.</p></li>
<li><p id="P31"><strong>Task Execution</strong> (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M20" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>E</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>) — The active phase of performing the communication task while simultaneously managing the tracking task. This state captures neuronal firing patterns and inter-group communication associated with task-switching and motor-related cognitive function.</p></li>
<li><p id="P32"><strong>Task Recovery</strong> (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M21" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>R</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>) — The transition period following task completion but before responding to the next event. This state reflects the brain’s shift from a high-workload multitasking condition to a lower workload demand, potentially revealing changes in neuronal communication patterns.</p></li>
<li><p id="P33"><strong>Task Other</strong> (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M22" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>O</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>) — The state in which participants were exposed to an audio cue but their assigned call sign was not mentioned. In this scenario, they were required to ignore the cue and maintain the current radio frequency settings. This state represents a passive listening condition where participants remained attentive but did not need to execute an action, providing a contrast to the multitasking nature of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M23" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>A</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>.</p></li>
</ul></section></section><section id="S11"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Methods: Proposed algorithms to characterize neuronal dynamics</h2>
<p id="P34">This section outlines the methods used to evaluate the neuronal oscillatory bands over time, as motivated by <a href="#T1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>. The EEG waveform is decomposed using an optimized filter bank approach (<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Napoli et al., 2020</a>, <a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">2017</a>), which forms the basis of the various proposed algorithms. This allows us to compare the different types of information (e.g., temporal, spectral, variance of oscillatory groups, hierarchical arrangements) required to decode the neural oscillations. <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a> provides a high-level overview of the methodological framework used to decode neuronal oscillations and evaluate the proposed algorithms.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F6"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 6.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a0fb/12991868/657fb99183c4/nihms-2140314-f0006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="730" width="1564" alt="Fig. 6."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P92">Overview of the proposed EEG feature extraction and evaluation framework. Raw EEG is decomposed into 12 intensity bands using an optimized filter bank. These form the basis of four algorithmic strategies (SI, CSI, SS, SIS), each evaluated using identical NAPS-based models under controlled conditions.</p></figcaption></figure><section id="S12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Preprocessing with established filter bank for spectral intensity extraction</h3>
<p id="P35">We deployed the proposed filter, as detailed in <a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Napoli et al. (2020</a>, <a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">2017</a>), to extract each EEG frequency band and analyze the continuous time–frequency intensity dynamics of EEG signals. This design was chosen because it ensures accurate extraction of each EEG frequency band while maintaining appropriate filter bank plateau values. To meet these and other constraints, a flattened Gaussian basis function (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M24" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ψ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>) was employed (<a href="#R5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R5">Blázquez et al., 2008</a>). The basis function that creates each <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M25" display="inline"><mrow><mi>i</mi><mtext>th</mtext></mrow></math></span> filter of the filter bank is defined as
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD1"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M1" display="block"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ψ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>f</mi><mo>;</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mrow><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>e</mi><mi>α</mi></msup><mo>−</mo><msup><mi>e</mi><mi>β</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>⋅</mo><mstyle mathvariant="italic"><mi>Θ</mi></mstyle><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(1)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M26" display="inline"><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>=</mo><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mrow><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>f</mi><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mrow><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo>,</mo><mi>β</mi><mo>=</mo><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mrow><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>f</mi><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mrow><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mn>4</mn></msup></mrow></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M27" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>f</mi><mrow><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> is the center frequency, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M28" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M29" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>b</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> are the tuning parameters, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M30" display="inline"><mrow><mstyle mathvariant="italic"><mi>Θ</mi></mstyle><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> is the Heaviside function to constrain the filter to only positive frequencies (<a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">Napoli et al., 2017</a>). The basis function parameters were optimized to ensure the filter bank uniformly represented all EEG frequencies while maintaining reasonable separability between the frequency bands. The optimized parameters are summarized in <a href="#T2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>, including the center frequencies (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M31" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>f</mi><mrow><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>), weights (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M32" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M33" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>b</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>), and lower and upper cutoff frequencies (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M34" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>F</mi><mrow><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M35" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>F</mi><mrow><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>) for each filter. Once designed in the frequency domain, the filters are transformed into the time domain using the Inverse Fourier Transform, followed by a circular shift (<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Napoli et al., 2020</a>), resulting in <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M36" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ψ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>. The EEG signal, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M37" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>s</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span> is then convolved with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M38" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ψ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span> producing <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M39" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>ρ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>, where each <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M40" display="inline"><mi>i</mi></math></span> output is associated with its respective <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M41" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ψ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>. This spectral output intensity, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M42" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>ρ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>, is then convolved with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M43" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>, a Gaussian Smoothing Filter,
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD2"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M2" display="block"><mrow><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><msqrt><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><msup><mi>σ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></msqrt></mfrac><msup><mi>e</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.5</mn><mrow><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="1"><mfrac><mi>x</mi><mi>σ</mi></mfrac></mstyle><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mrow></msup><mo>.</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(2)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M44" display="inline"><mrow><mi>σ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>F</mi><mi>s</mi></msub></mrow><mn>2</mn></mfrac></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M45" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>F</mi><mi>s</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> is the sampling frequency and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M46" display="inline"><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>=</mo><mstyle mathvariant="normal"><mo>∈</mo></mstyle><mspace width="thinmathspace"></mspace><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="1"><mfrac><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>3</mn><msub><mi>F</mi><mi>s</mi></msub></mrow><mn>2</mn></mfrac></mstyle><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="1"><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><msub><mi>F</mi><mi>s</mi></msub></mrow><mn>2</mn></mfrac></mstyle><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></math></span>. This produces a smoothed spectral output intensity, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M47" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>, where the details of the full process are described in <a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Napoli et al. (2020)</a>.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="S13"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. Proposed algorithms for EEG dynamics analysis</h3>
<p id="P36">This subsection presents four main conceptual approaches for decomposing and evaluating the dynamics of hierarchical arrangements within a critical event generated by these neuronal oscillatory bands: (1) Spectral Intensity (SI) in <a href="#S14" class="usa-link">Section 3.2.1</a>; (2) Variations of Competing Spectral Intensity Measurements (CSI) in <a href="#S15" class="usa-link">Section 3.2.2</a>; (3) Spectral Stability (SS) in <a href="#S18" class="usa-link">Section 3.2.3</a>; and Spectral Intensity Stability (SIS) is <a href="#S19" class="usa-link">Section 3.2.4</a>.</p>
<section id="S14"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.1. Filter bank bands: “spectral intensity (SI)”</h4>
<p id="P37">Spectral intensity is a standard metric of EEG interpretation, we use this first measure as the baseline or control to compare to the other methods. Utilizing the foundational EEG filter bank, we can calculate the total intensity for each spectral band and for each task (e.g., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M48" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>). This is accomplished by first defining the window, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M49" display="inline"><mi>τ</mi></math></span>, for each task from the MATB file, which is delineated by the start time, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M50" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>s</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, and a completion time, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M51" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>c</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> of the task, described in <a href="#S10" class="usa-link">Section 2.3</a>. The total spectral intensity is calculated by,
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD3"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M3" display="block"><mrow><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>I</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>τ</mi></munderover><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(3)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M52" display="inline"><mi>i</mi></math></span> is the filter.</p></section><section id="S15"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2. Competing Spectral Intensity (CSI)</h4>
<p id="P38">The Engagement Index (EI) was introduced by Pope et al. as a psychophysiological measure that indicates the level of engagement of the user in a task based on the proportional power of the alpha, beta, and theta EEG bands (<a href="#R31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R31">Pope et al., 1995</a>). The underlying principle is based on the frequency band interpretation: the beta EEG frequency band is linked to concentration, active thinking, and arousal. Thus, beta is set proportionally at the numerator, while the EEG frequency bands of delta and theta, which are linked to sleep and inhibition, are set at the denominator. This allows us to compare the competing nature between EEG frequency bands through proportional changes. This work has been extensively used in literature to monitor human engagement during tasks, which has been related to workload as task load increases (<a href="#R32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R32">Prinzel et al., 2000</a>).</p>
<p id="P39">In this section, we introduce the classical approach of utilizing the EI, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M53" display="inline"><mi>E</mi></math></span>, which allows us to compare the proportional nature of frequency bands but fails to provide information about how spectral intensity dynamics compete and change over time. We then propose a new dynamic method for the EI that allows us to capture the stochastic nature of the EEG’s spectral intensity dynamics over time in a more granular way, denoted as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M54" display="inline"><mrow><msup><mi>𝒟</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M55" display="inline"><mrow><msup><mi>𝒟</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span> is explored over two different ranges of beta and then described using summary statistics of sum (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M56" display="inline"><mstyle mathvariant="italic"><mi>Σ</mi></mstyle></math></span>), mean (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M57" display="inline"><mi>μ</mi></math></span>), and standard deviation (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M58" display="inline"><mi>σ</mi></math></span>).</p>
<section id="S16"><h5 class="pmc_sec_title">Proportional CSI (pCSI)</h5>
<p id="P40">The Classical EI was the first algorithm introduced by Pope. It is calculated using the proportion of beta over the sum of alpha and theta waves (<a href="#R31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R31">Pope et al., 1995</a>), where we refer to this as Proportional CSI (pCSI). The EI defined over a window of time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M59" display="inline"><mi>τ</mi></math></span> is defined as,
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD4"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M4" display="block"><mrow><mi>E</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>τ</mi></msubsup></mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>5</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>+</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>6</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>+</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>7</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>τ</mi></msubsup></mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>+</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>3</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>+</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>4</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mfrac><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(4)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M60" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> is the output of the filter bank. This approach showed good results in defining the overall engagement of an individual over a time window. However, when the time window becomes too large, multiple cognitive states may occur and attention may vary. Therefore, this approach is limited due to a lack of temporal information that specifies how engaged an individual was at a specific point in time.</p></section><section id="S17"><h5 class="pmc_sec_title">Stochastic CSI (sCSI)</h5>
<p id="P41">Expanding the EI analysis by examining how engagement dynamically alters over time, we utilize the mean and standard deviation to capture the stochastic nature of these oscillatory bands, where we refer to this as Stochastic CSI (sCSI). As mentioned previously, the original EI method is a summation of the intensity over a window of time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M61" display="inline"><mi>τ</mi></math></span>. As a result, it does not provide the temporal dynamics of the EEG frequency bands within the window of time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M62" display="inline"><mi>τ</mi></math></span>, only the overall engagement. To address this, we propose a dynamic engagement approach to the EI, defined as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M63" display="inline"><mrow><msup><mi>𝒟</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>, which is an instantaneous measurement of the EEG intensity found within the filter bank outputs, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M64" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>. For each point <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M65" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> in time, the EI is quantified as follows:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD5"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M5" display="block"><mrow><msup><mi>𝒟</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow><mi>j</mi></msubsup></mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>+</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>3</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>+</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>4</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mfrac><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(5)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where k and j are the lower limit and upper limit intensity filter banks, respectively. This change in the calculation provides an instantaneous overview of EI changes over time within the window <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M66" display="inline"><mi>τ</mi></math></span>.</p>
<p id="P42">The definition of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M67" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>𝒟</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> is expanded to characterize the dynamics of the EI over a window of time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M68" display="inline"><mi>τ</mi></math></span>. The sum, mean, and standard deviation of the EI over this window of time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M69" display="inline"><mi>τ</mi></math></span> are described as:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD6"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M6" display="block"><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>𝒟</mi><mstyle mathvariant="italic"><mi>Σ</mi></mstyle><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup></mrow><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>τ</mi></munderover><msup><mi>𝒟</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(6)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD7"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M7" display="block"><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>𝒟</mi><mi>μ</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup></mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>τ</mi></mfrac><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>τ</mi></munderover><msup><mi>𝒟</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(7)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD8"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M8" display="block"><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>𝒟</mi><mi>σ</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup></mrow><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mrow><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>τ</mi></mfrac><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>τ</mi></munderover><mrow><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>𝒟</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>−</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>𝒟</mi><mi>μ</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mrow></msqrt><mo>.</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(8)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P43">Both <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M70" display="inline"><mrow><msubsup><mi>𝒟</mi><mi>μ</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M71" display="inline"><mrow><msubsup><mi>𝒟</mi><mi>σ</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup></mrow></math></span> are similar to the original engagement index <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M72" display="inline"><mi>E</mi></math></span> in that they are static measurements of task engagement within a specified window of time. However, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M73" display="inline"><mrow><msubsup><mi>𝒟</mi><mstyle mathvariant="italic"><mi>Σ</mi></mstyle><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup></mrow></math></span> differs because the summation provides a more dynamic measurement of task engagement, capturing the total task engagement seen within a window of time. <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a> depicts an example of how CSI is calculated. The proposed method explores two different frequency ranges for the numerator, but algorithmically, various hierarchical arrangements should be further investigated. Our first frequency bands utilized within the numerator are the original frequency ranges applied for the classical EI, which are 13–22 Hz and denoted as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M74" display="inline"><mi>𝒟</mi></math></span><sup><a href="#R5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R5">5</a>,<a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">7</a></sup>. An extended frequency range is also explored, which encompasses the low beta, mid beta, high beta, low gamma, and high gamma spectral intensity bands. This second hierarchical arrangement extends the frequency range from 13–22 Hz to the range of 13–38 Hz. This extended range is defined as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M75" display="inline"><mi>𝒟</mi></math></span><sup><a href="#R5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R5">5</a>,<a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">12</a></sup>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F7"><h6 class="obj_head">Fig. 7. Step-by-step visualization of the Competing Spectral Intensity (CSI) analysis.</h6>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a0fb/12991868/f7b066a7f998/nihms-2140314-f0007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="925" width="1693" alt="Fig. 7."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P93">The top panel shows the raw EEG signal over time. The middle panel displays the spectral intensity of the alpha (red), beta (blue), and theta (green) frequency bands, which are used to compute CSI. The middle-left panel presents the summed spectral intensity of these bands over three non-overlapping 5-second time windows, highlighting their proportional contributions. The bottom-left panel shows the Proportional CSI (pCSI) across each time window, representing a static measure of engagement. The middle-right panel depicts the Stochastic CSI (sCSI) over time, capturing the dynamic fluctuations in engagement. The bottom-right panel summarizes the sum, mean, and standard deviation of sCSI across each 5-second time window. To ensure better visualization, the sum of sCSI has been scaled down by a factor of 1000 to match the scale of the mean and standard deviation.</p></figcaption></figure></section></section><section id="S18"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.3. Oscillations group stability “Spectral Stability (SS)”</h4>
<p id="P44">Spectral Stability (SS) quantifies how stable or predictable the hierarchical arrangements of neuronal oscillations are over time. We evaluate SS using an entropy-based metric called Temporal Cross-Band Entropy (TCBE), which measures the disorder or unpredictability in the rank order of spectral intensities across different EEG frequency bands.</p>
<p id="P45">Building on the foundational EEG filter bank, specific hierarchical arrangements can be identified through rank-order patterns at each time instance, enabling evaluation of the unique dynamics of oscillatory group arrangements. This algorithm is versatile and can be applied to time series data of varying lengths. Instead of treating the signal as a single one-dimensional sequence, it is analyzed as a matrix, where each row corresponds to an output of the filter bank, and each column represents a time instance. For the proposed filter bank, we have <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M76" display="inline"><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>12</mn></mrow></math></span> filters and a given time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M77" display="inline"><mrow><mtext>series</mtext><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>s</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>, we produce <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M78" display="inline"><mi>M</mi></math></span> intensity sequences, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M79" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M80" display="inline"><mrow><mstyle mathvariant="normal"><mo>∀</mo></mstyle><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M81" display="inline"><mrow><mstyle mathvariant="normal"><mo>∀</mo></mstyle><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></math></span>. This allows us to form an intensity matrix,
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD9"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M9" display="block"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">P</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="true">[</mo><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mo>⋯</mo></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mo>⋯</mo></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="center"><mo>⋮</mo></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mo>⋮</mo></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mo>⋱</mo></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mo>⋮</mo></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mo>⋯</mo></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>M</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>M</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mo>⋯</mo></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>M</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable><mo stretchy="true">]</mo></mrow><mo>.</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(9)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P46">Similar to how all other entropy measurements examine a vector sequence with a defined template length (<a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">Lake et al., 2002</a>) or an embedded dimension (<a href="#R48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R48">Zanin et al., 2012</a>), our embedded dimension is defined by the number of filters in the filter bank, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M82" display="inline"><mi>M</mi></math></span>. Thus we examine <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M83" display="inline"><mi>N</mi></math></span> column vectors composed from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M84" display="inline"><mi mathvariant="bold">P</mi></math></span>, which consist of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M85" display="inline"><mi>M</mi></math></span> subsequent intensity values. Each column vector,
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD10"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M10" display="block"><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>M</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mi>T</mi></msup></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(10)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
has a specific rank order pattern which is defined as a unique hierarchical arrangement (permutation), <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M86" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>π</mi><mi>C</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>≜</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><msub><mi>r</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>r</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>r</mi><mi>M</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span> of the form (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M87" display="inline"><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></math></span>), which fulfills
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD11"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M11" display="block"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>≤</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>≤</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>≤</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>M</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(11)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M88" display="inline"><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>1</mn><mspace width="thinmathspace"></mspace><mi>…</mi><mi>M</mi><mo>!</mo><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M89" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>π</mi><mi>C</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> equal of total number of occurrences of that hierarchical arrangement, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M90" display="inline"><mi>C</mi></math></span>. For clarification, a numerical example is provided, starting with the intensity matrix,

</tr></table>
<p>
where the embedded dimension is <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M91" display="inline"><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></math></span> and the length of the time series is <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M92" display="inline"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>8</mn></mrow></math></span>. In this case, the matrix <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M93" display="inline"><mi mathvariant="bold">P</mi></math></span> produces multiple unique hierarchical arrangements. For example:
