<p id="P47">Thus, the total number of unique hierarchical arrangements that can be achieved is <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M94" display="inline"><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>!</mo><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>!</mo><mo>=</mo><mn>24</mn></mrow></math></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M95" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>π</mi><mi>C</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> equals the number of occurrences for each unique hierarchical arrangement: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M96" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>π</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M97" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>π</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M98" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>π</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M99" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>π</mi><mn>8</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M100" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>π</mi><mn>12</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>, and others= 0.</p>
<p id="P48">From these unique hierarchical arrangements, we evaluate spectral stability using entropy, a measure of disorder or predictability in time series data. Traditional entropy methods are often used in nonlinear systems to assess complexity or stability but are primarily designed for time-domain analysis rather than spectral information (<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Delgado-Bonal and Marshak, 2019</a>; <a href="#R41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R41">Song et al., 2010</a>; <a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Napoli et al., 2020</a>). To address this, we introduce Temporal Cross-Band Entropy (TCBE), an entropy-based method tailored to quantify spectral stability by analyzing how the hierarchical arrangements of neuronal oscillations evolve over time. Unlike conventional entropy measures, TCBE accounts for both spectral and temporal dynamics while disregarding absolute intensity values. TCBE evaluates how spectral bands interact by tracking their rank order over time. When the rank order remains stable, entropy is low, indicating high spectral stability. Conversely, frequent changes in rank order result in higher entropy, reflecting increased variability in spectral organization. Unlike conventional entropy measures, TCBE operates in a two-dimensional space and does not require a predefined template length (<a href="#R48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R48">Zanin et al., 2012</a>; <a href="#R24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R24">Napoli et al., 2018</a>). The dynamics of spectral intensity rank changes analyzed through TCBE are illustrated in <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F8"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 8. Step-by-Step Illustration of Spectral Stability (SS) Analysis.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a0fb/12991868/4f93ba72f07c/nihms-2140314-f0008.jpg" loading="lazy" height="917" width="1655" alt="Fig. 8."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P94">This figure demonstrates the calculation of spectral stability by tracking the hierarchical ordering of EEG frequency bands over time. The top panel presents the raw EEG signal. The second panel shows the intensity of the alpha, beta, and theta bands, which form the basis of the stability analysis. The third panel displays the hierarchical rank order of these frequency bands, where each unique permutation is assigned a numerical label and plotted as a step function. The bottom panel shows TCBE, which quantifies spectral stability by measuring the variability of rank-order transitions over a time window. The zero values at the beginning of the TCBE signal illustrate that TCBE is computed over a window of time (5 s in this example), meaning entropy values cannot be determined for the first few seconds of data. Higher TCBE values indicate greater instability in spectral ordering, while lower values reflect more stable hierarchical arrangements of EEG frequency bands.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P49">The TCBE metric, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M101" display="inline"><mi>𝒳</mi></math></span>, is computed as:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD14"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M14" display="block"><mrow><mi>𝒳</mi><mo>=</mo><mo>−</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mrow><msub><mi>log</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>M</mi><mo>!</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mfrac><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>!</mo></mrow></munderover><msub><mi>π</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mspace width="thinmathspace"></mspace><msub><mi>log</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>π</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>.</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(13)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P50">The notation <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M102" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>𝒳</mi><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>:</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> was coined to describe the TCBE across the range of filters from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M103" display="inline"><mi>a</mi></math></span> to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M104" display="inline"><mi>b</mi></math></span>, with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M105" display="inline"><mi>M</mi></math></span> the total number of filters. We also coin the notation <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M106" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>𝒳</mi><mrow><mo>∑</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>c</mi><mo>:</mo><mi>d</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>:</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> where in addition to filters <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M107" display="inline"><mi>a</mi></math></span> to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M108" display="inline"><mi>b</mi></math></span>, we include an additional composite filter of the summation of filters <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M109" display="inline"><mi>c</mi></math></span> to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M110" display="inline"><mi>d</mi></math></span>. In either case, the intensity matrix <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M111" display="inline"><mi mathvariant="bold">P</mi></math></span> would be manipulated to account for this new range. Thus, each column vector, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M112" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, of the intensity matrix would be defined as,
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD15"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M15" display="block"><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>a</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mrow><mi>b</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>b</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>.</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(14)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P51">For example, when we declare <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M113" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>𝒳</mi><mrow><mn>5</mn><mo>:</mo><mn>12</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, it means we applied <a href="#FD14" class="usa-link">Eq. (13)</a> to the unique hierarchical arrangements found in the intensity matrix
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD16"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M16" display="block"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">P</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="true">[</mo><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="center"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mn>12</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable><mo stretchy="true">]</mo></mrow><mo>.</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(15)</td>
</tr></table></section><section id="S19"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.4. TCBE <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M114" display="inline"><mi>x</mi></math></span> SI (“Spectral Intensity Stability (SIS))”</h4>
<p id="P52">Spectral Intensity Stability (SIS) captures the relationship between spectral intensity and spectral stability, providing insights into how the strength and arrangement of oscillatory bands evolve over time. SIS is computed as a weighted interaction between TCBE and spectral intensity:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD17"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M17" display="block"><mrow><mi>S</mi><mi>I</mi><msub><mi>S</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1.01</mn><mo>−</mo><mi>T</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>B</mi><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>⋅</mo><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>I</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(16)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M115" display="inline"><mrow><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>I</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> represents the spectral intensity of the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M116" display="inline"><mrow><mi>i</mi><mtext>th</mtext></mrow></math></span> frequency band, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M117" display="inline"><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>B</mi><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> quantifies the spectral stability at time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M118" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span>. The factor 1.01 is introduced because TCBE is bounded between 0 and 1, ensuring that SIS does not become excessively large when TCBE values are very small, maintaining a more balanced measure of spectral intensity stability.</p>
<p id="P53">One of the key strengths of SIS is its high temporal resolution. Since EEG was sampled at 256 Hz in this study, each time step <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M119" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> corresponds to approximately 3.91 ms. This allows SIS to capture rapid fluctuations in neural oscillatory hierarchies that may be missed by traditional sliding-window spectral analysis. By updating TCBE and spectral intensity at each sample, SIS offers a fine-grained view of neural dynamics, making it especially useful for detecting short-lived critical transitions in cognitive state.</p>
<p id="P54">To manage the complexity introduced by the numerous possible hierarchical arrangements, we focus on the most predictive spectral stability features, specifically <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M120" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>𝒳</mi><mrow><mn>5</mn><mo>:</mo><mn>12</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, which corresponds to beta and gamma bands, frequencies closely associated with cognitive processes. However, SIS can be extended to various frequency combinations, making it a versatile tool for future research on neuronal dynamics (see <a href="#T3" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>).</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section></section></section><section id="S20"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Methods: Evaluating EEG feature families for cognitive task prediction</h2>
<section id="S21"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1. Controlled feature evaluation for explainable cognitive state detection</h3>
<p id="P55">To systematically evaluate the discriminative power of our proposed EEG-based algorithmic strategies, we performed a controlled feature evaluation, analogous to an ablation study. Each algorithmic strategy was assessed under identical experimental conditions to ensure a fair and interpretable comparison. Specifically, we built four independent models, each using features exclusively from one of the proposed feature families (SI, CSI, SS, or SIS). All models shared the same machine learning architecture based on the NAPS framework and were restricted to the same number of features (N) drawn from their respective families. The NAPS framework was utilized as the ML control, as it has been shown to overcome numerous challenges in human experiments, providing a more reliable baseline assessment across feature families. Additionally, to ensure the accuracy of these comparative assessments, all experiments used identical dataset splits, class label definitions, and evaluation protocols. This controlled setup allowed us to isolate the contribution of each feature family and quantify its predictive utility for cognitive state classification. Model performance was evaluated using the F1-Score and the Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC) (<a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">Chicco and Jurman, 2020</a>; <a href="#R23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R23">MATLAB, 2020</a>; <a href="#R44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R44">The MathWorks Inc., 2020</a>). A visual summary of this evaluation framework is provided in <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a>.</p>
<p id="P56">The classification problem was based on task-related cognitive states derived from MATB-II log file decomposition (detailed in <a href="#S10" class="usa-link">Section 2.3</a>), resulting in five distinct classes: Task Precursor (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M121" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, 411 samples), Task Interruption (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M122" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>A</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, 411), Task Execution (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M123" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>E</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, 411), Task Recovery (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M124" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>R</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, 411), and Task Other (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M125" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>O</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, 460). To incorporate the effects of cognitive impairment, each class was further split into hypoxia (H) and non-hypoxia (NH) conditions, forming a ten-class problem. The hypoxia conditions included 212 samples each for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M126" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M127" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>A</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M128" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>E</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M129" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>R</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M130" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>O</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> comprising 230 samples. The non-hypoxia counterparts had 199 samples each for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M131" display="inline"><mrow><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M132" display="inline"><mrow><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>A</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M133" display="inline"><mrow><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>E</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M134" display="inline"><mrow><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>R</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, and 230 samples for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M135" display="inline"><mrow><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>O</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>.</p></section><section id="S22"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.2. NAPS-Augmented Machine Learning Framework</h3>
<p id="P57">The machine learning model used in this study is the NAPS Augmented ML Framework, originally developed to support physiological data analysis such as EEG (<a href="#R27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R27">Napoli et al., 2022</a>). NAPS is designed for tabular feature data and has an architecture capable of handling the ML challenges of human experimental data, including low sample sizes, class imbalance, and subject-to-subject variability. This provides a strong, consistent model for comparing families of features, improving the ability to model subtle variations across cognitive states and making it well-suited for identifying which features drive classification performance. It includes two components particularly useful for this work: augmented response variable mapping and a sensor model paradigm, both of which improve the ability to model subtle variations across cognitive states. While we did not use the full NAPS pipeline (e.g., fusion and uncertainty weighting), we implemented its core structure: the all-versus-one augmented response mapping and the sensor model architecture. These components were used to generate hierarchical combinations of response variables and enable robust modeling across subject-specific data subsets. After applying the sensor model paradigm, we trained models using Bootstrap Aggregated Decision Trees (TreeBagger in MATLAB) (<a href="#R23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R23">MATLAB, 2020</a>), which aggregate multiple decision trees created via Breiman’s Random Forest algorithm (<a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">Breiman, 2001</a>). This ensemble approach improves generalizability and reduces overfitting, especially in high-variance physiological data. Voting across the sensor models was used to derive final predictions. To handle class imbalance, especially in the binary augmented models, the Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE) was applied during training. An example of how NAPS generates augmented binary models from a five-class structure is shown in <a href="#T4" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>. To answer RQ2, feature inputs were restricted to a single algorithmic strategy per model to evaluate which feature family (SI, CSI, SS, or SIS) best captured task-related cognitive state transitions. Each sensor model used a fixed number of features from one family, and the final classification was based on the 16 best-performing models with the least associated uncertainty.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section></section><section id="S23"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">5. Results</h2>
<p id="P58">To determine which neuronal firing dynamics contain the most information for cognitive state detection, we examine the four proposed families of features to evaluate the most discriminative for the detection of cognitive task states and impairment (i.e., hypoxia). These families of features all use the same foundational spectral decomposition method but process the decoding of the activation and interaction of neural oscillatory bands differently. This decoding is conceptually focused on evaluating how the intensity dynamics of neural frequency-based oscillations alter or are arranged hierarchically over time. These nascent methodological approaches provide a critical foundation for understanding underlying dynamics and processes of criticality concerning cognition that have been neglected. This evidence supporting the specific mechanics of neuronal firing is through the information extracted in order to discriminate cognitive states which are substantiated by assessing the predictive performance of the ML model (discussed in <a href="#S22" class="usa-link">Section 4.2</a>) through a comparison of models using various contextual features that characterize EEG dynamics in four primary dimensions: (1) Spectral Intensity, (2) Spectral Stability, (3) Competing Spectral Intensity, and (4) Spectral Intensity Stability. The optimal predictive model identifies which of these features offers the most distinguishing and informative EEG dynamics for accurately predicting cognitive processes in task classification. In our reporting of the ML’s performance, we provide two summary metrics: the MCC and F1 scores. In addition, four supporting metrics provide further insight into the performance and errors: false positives (FP), false negatives (FN), true positives (TP), and true negatives (TN). The FPs, also known as the false alarm, occur when the class is classified as positive even when it is not. The FNs, also known as the miss classification, occur when the classification model fails to detect a true positive.</p>
<p id="P59">To ensure the validity of cognitive state classification, ground truth labels were established based on a structured decomposition of MATB output logs (see <a href="#S10" class="usa-link">Section 2.3</a>). The MATB system provides precise event markers, including task initiation, user responses, and task completion timestamps, which were used to define transitions between cognitive states: Task Precursor (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M136" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>), Audio Task Interruption (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M137" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>A</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>), Task Execution (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M138" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>E</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>), Task Recovery (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M139" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>R</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>), and Task Other (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M140" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>O</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>). These transitions were objectively determined using system-generated logs, ensuring that state definitions were not based on subjective criteria. Additionally, timestamps from these task events were aligned with EEG data to verify that neural activity patterns corresponded to distinct cognitive states.</p>
<section id="S24"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">5.1. RQ1: Decoding neuronal dynamics related to task classification</h3>
<p id="P60">Our first research question is “Do unique dynamics within these task events that elicit criticality have unique dynamics, potentially establishing a “fingerprint” for cognitive processes?”. We first present the predictive model’s performance for the merged cohorts of the hypoxia and non-hypoxia datasets for the 5-class problem across each algorithmic strategy.</p>
<p id="P61">The summary metrics of MMC and F1-Score across algorithmic strategies for the 5-class model are depicted in <a href="#F9" class="usa-link">Fig. 9</a>, where the traditional <em>SI algorithm</em> achieves an overall MCC of 0.52 and is the lowest performing model presented within the set. The <em>CSI algorithmic strategy</em> exhibits an MCC of 0.63 for the 5-class classification task, providing a predictive power increase of 21.15% compared to the SI. The SS algorithm achieves an MCC of 0.54 with a 3.85% increase compared to the SI algorithm and a 16.67% decrease compared to the CSI algorithm. However, most importantly, the <em>SIS algorithmic strategy</em>, shows the best performance with an MCC of 0.68 with an increase of 7.93% when compared to the CSI, a 20.58% increase when compared to SS and 23.52% increase compared to the traditional SI algorithm.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F9"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a0fb/12991868/dcd5cc10771a/nihms-2140314-f0009.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1427" width="1692" alt="Fig. 9."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F9/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P95">The four confusion matrices (CM) compare the predictive performance of the following methods: (1) classical spectral intensity (SI) analysis, which provides intensity information without incorporating temporal dynamics; (2) the proposed dynamic engagement index, which measures the stochastic properties of spectral intensity dynamics; (3) the proposed TCBE, which captures spectral temporal dynamics while excluding intensity information; and (4) the interaction of TCBE with spectral intensity, representing temporal spectral intensity dynamics.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P62"><a href="#T5" class="usa-link">Table 5</a> exhibits a breakdown of each algorithm’s performance using the true positives (TP), true negatives (TN), false positives (FP), and true negatives (TN) across the five classes. This table allows us to evaluate if a unique algorithmic strategy can discriminate across a specific cognitive task and how it performs overall. It is worth noting that SIS dominating performs across all tasks and for all performance metrics (e.g., TP, TN) except for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M141" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>O</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>. The CSI algorithm best predicts the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M142" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></math></span>, with an increase of 18.16% of TPs compared to the second-best algorithm, SIS. Although the worst-performing algorithm for predicting TPs is SI, SS performs only slightly better.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="T5"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 5.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p id="P101">This table compares all induced tasks across the four algorithmic strategies, presenting the number of True Positives, False Negatives, False Positives, and True Negatives. The highlighted cells provide a visual comparison of performance: dark green indicates the best performance, light green denotes the second-best performer, orange marks the second-to-worst performer, and red highlights the worst performer. This coloring scheme aids in identifying which algorithmic strategy is most favorable for specific tasks.</p></div>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="S25"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">5.2. RQ2: Decoding neuronal dynamics related to impairment and task classification</h3>
<p id="P63">From the perspective of cognitive impairment, we alter the response variable to address a 10-class cognitive task prediction problem, allowing us to expand the research question of “Does the depression of neuronal firing due to hypoxia lead to events of criticality with unique spectral arrangements?”, based on the tasks shown in <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>.</p>
<p id="P64">The 10-class model provides summary metrics that yielded an MCC of 0.54 for the <em>SS algorithmic strategy</em>, which was the worst-performing algorithm. The SI algorithm does slightly better with an MCC of .56, providing an increase of 3.57% in performance. The CSI approach provides an MCC of .60, which provides a performance increase of 7.14% and 11.11% compared to the SI and SS, respectively. The best-performing algorithm demonstrates superior discrimination with an MCC of 0.727, which is a 34.62% increase in performance compared to the worst algorithm SS and a 29.82% increase in performance to the traditional SI approach.</p>
<p id="P65">We provide <a href="#T6" class="usa-link">Table 6</a> to further break down each algorithm’s performances using the TP, NP, FP, and TN for each of the ten classes. Similar to the 5-class model, the SIS algorithmic strategy is the best discriminator across all classes, except for the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M143" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>O</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M144" display="inline"><mrow><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>O</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>. Thus, the discrimination performance using SIS seems similar to the 5-class model and has limitations with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M145" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>O</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> as a whole. CSI remains the top-performing approach for distinguishing the specific class <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M146" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>O</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, regardless of whether it is a 5-class or 10-class model. Although the CSI algorithm had the second-best MCC when compared to the other approaches, the majority of the second-best TP classified featured are located within the SI algorithm strategy (i.e., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M147" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M148" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>A</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M149" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>E</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M150" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>R</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M151" display="inline"><mrow><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>A</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M152" display="inline"><mrow><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>R</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>). Regarding true positives and false negatives, SI, previously one of the weakest performers, has shown notable improvement and is now on the verge of becoming the second-best performer. SI’s performance hurdles and limitations are due to the performance in the false positive and true negative rates.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="T6"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 6.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p id="P102">This table compares all induced tasks and hypoxia conditions across the four algorithmic strategies, presenting the number of True Positives, False Negatives, False Positives, and True Negatives. The highlighted cells provide a visual comparison of performance: dark green indicates the best performance, light green denotes the second-best performer, orange marks the second-to-worst performer, and red highlights the worst performer. This coloring scheme aids in identifying which algorithmic strategy is most favorable for specific tasks.</p></div>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section></section><section id="S26"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">6. Discussion</h2>
<p id="P66">In earlier sections, we theorized, based on Newell’s timescale, that cognitive processes unfold across multiple temporal levels, and that analyzing EEG dynamics at granular, millisecond-scale intervals provides important information about the behavior of neuronal oscillatory groups. These dynamics can be captured through summated intensity, proportional shifts, stochastic fluctuations, or entropic measures that assess the stability of interactions among oscillatory components. Each of the four algorithms we introduced – Spectral Intensity (SI), Spectral Stability (SS), Competing Spectral Intensity (CSI), and Spectral Intensity Stability (SIS) – captures a distinct yet complementary aspect of these spectral dynamics. While previous work has largely focused on SI (<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Napoli et al., 2020</a>), our study introduces three new feature families – SS, CSI, and SIS – that aim to characterize how oscillatory activity changes over time during task-based cognitive processing. These new approaches go beyond traditional power analysis by quantifying not only the strength of specific frequency bands but also how their relationships shift, compete, and reorganize in response to task demands.</p>
<p id="P67">The entropic methods introduced in SS and SIS quantify the stability of spectral hierarchies by tracking how the relative ranking of frequency bands evolves over time. This provides a new perspective on how neural systems coordinate activity across multiple frequencies to support cognitive functions. <strong><em>We propose that these findings support a new conceptual model for cognitive state detection: Granular Analysis Informing Neural Stability (GAINS).</em></strong> GAINS highlights the idea that tracking fine-scale changes in spectral structure, rather than relying on coarse averages of power, offers more meaningful insight into the organization and dynamics of brain activity. We do not claim that our specific implementations, such as TCBE or the dynamic engagement index, represent the final or ideal solution. Instead, we view them as first steps in operationalizing the GAINS principle. Our broader goal is to motivate the field toward exploring how fine-grained spectral dynamics can improve EEG-based cognitive state classification.</p>
<p id="P68">More specifically, our proposed algorithms reflect the GAINS concept by examining how spectral intensities form evolving hierarchical patterns over time. These changes, illustrated in <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>, appear to encode task-relevant cognitive transitions. In simpler terms, the moment-to-moment self-organization of frequency bands seems to correspond with the functional demands of different tasks. To explore this idea, we presented four algorithmic strategies, each offering a unique view into EEG spectral activity. Together, these algorithms represent a progressive improvement in capturing informative features, incorporating granularity, temporal dynamics, and spectral relationships to enhance our ability to interpret brain activity.</p>
<p id="P69">The <em>task classification</em> and <em>cognitive impairment task classification</em> models demonstrated comparable predictive power between the traditional SI algorithm and the SS algorithm. It is important to note that these algorithms rely on fundamentally different bases of information. Spectral Stability measures hierarchical arrangements of frequency bands, quantifying how often specific arrangements occur and their predictability at the circuit level. This measure, catenated to the resolution of operations, serves as an entropy metric capturing the stability of firing pattern arrangements without considering the intensity of spectral bands. This concept of discriminative information is visually depicted in <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>, where the stability dynamics are highlighted in the outer figures. Consequently, SS provides granular contextual information about neuronal circuit dynamics on a temporal timescale of approximately 3.9 ms (using a 256 Hz sampling rate), illustrating how fundamental building blocks collaborate to form cognitive processes. The entropy measured by SS appears to be task-specific, resulting in a unique criticality arrangement for each cognitive task.</p>
<p id="P70">In contrast, Spectral Intensity focuses on summed intensities over time, reflecting operational and unit task resolutions derived from independent frequency bands without accounting for their interactions. Spectral intensity can be calculated through two methods: the standard approach, which sums the power over specific frequency ranges obtained from a Fourier Transform (illustrated in the central figure of <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>), and a secondary approach, which sums the intensity of the bandpass filter, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M153" display="inline"><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>ρ</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>, over time. The latter method captures circuit-level intensities but loses granularity through temporal summation, resulting in information aggregated at the operations or unit task level. The strikingly similar performance of SI and SS for both the 5-class and 10-class problems highlights the critical importance of both spectral intensity and its dynamics (hierarchical arrangements) over time.</p>
<p id="P71">Building on this, we introduce the Competing Spectral Intensities (CSI) algorithm, which integrates spectral intensity and dynamic information from spectral bands but differs from SS in two key ways: (1) employing a less granular timescale and (2) using a coarse measure of spectral dynamics. CSI is conceptually analogous to a coarse combination of SI and SS, summarized at the operations and unit task level using classical statistics (mean and variance). By merging spectral intensity and frequency band dynamics, CSI achieves enhanced predictive power compared to algorithms that rely solely on one form of information. For the 5-class problem, CSI achieves an MCC of 0.63, reflecting a 21.1% improvement over SI and a 16.6% improvement over SS, as shown in <a href="#F9" class="usa-link">Fig. 9</a>. For the 10-class problem, CSI shows an 8.9% improvement over SI and a 12.9% improvement over SS, as seen in <a href="#F10" class="usa-link">Fig. 10</a>. In <a href="#T5" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>, CSI emerges as the second-best algorithm for most tasks (3 out of 5) when comparing True Positives (TP) and False Negatives (FN) across all algorithms, occasionally tying with SI. However, for the 10-class problem (<a href="#T6" class="usa-link">Table 6</a>), SI outperforms CSI in TP rates for 6 out of 10 tasks.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F10"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 10.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a0fb/12991868/dd4570b699e2/nihms-2140314-f0010.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1347" width="1609" alt="Fig. 10."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F10/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P96">The four confusion matrices (CM) compare the predictive performance of the following methods: (1) the classical intensity analysis, which provides intensity information without incorporating temporal dynamics; (2) the proposed dynamic engagement indices, representing the stochastic nature of spectral intensity dynamics over time; (3) the proposed TCBE, which captures the complexity of spectral temporal dynamics while excluding intensity information; and (4) the interaction of TCBE with spectral intensity, representing refined temporal spectral intensity dynamics</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P72">SIS introduces a novel approach for EEG-based cognitive state detection by combining instantaneous spectral intensity with entropy-based measurements of sample-by-sample spectral hierarchical arrangements. This dual-focus allows SIS to track not just how strong brain frequencies are, but how they organize over time, something that traditional methods overlook. Unlike CSI, which summarizes intensity changes over broader time windows, SIS operates at the resolution of individual EEG samples (3.91 ms at 256 Hz), making it uniquely sensitive to fast, transient shifts in brain dynamics. This fine-grained resolution enables SIS to detect meaningful reorganizations in the spectral structure of the brain, capturing subtle but critical patterns that are missed by classical approaches. This capability is not just a technical improvement, it addresses a fundamental challenge in cognitive neuroscience: brain states often change rapidly and involve not just fluctuations in power but shifts in how different frequency bands are coordinated. SIS is specifically designed to capture these reorganizations. Our results show the strength of SIS: it achieved the highest accuracy in both cohort-agnostic task prediction (30.7% improvement over SI, 25.9% over SS, and 7.9% over CSI) and cognitive impairment classification (29.8% over SI, 34.6% over SS, and 21.1% over CSI). These gains underscore the critical value of capturing both what spectral features are present and how they are structured and maintained over time. These results suggest that SIS provides uniquely informative features for decoding cognitive states and differentiating between hypoxic and normoxic conditions. The strong performance of SIS in these conditions suggests that oxygen deprivation alters the spectral organization of neuronal oscillations in ways that SIS is uniquely sensitive to. These findings raise the possibility that SIS could be applied to real-time cognitive monitoring in operational settings where hypoxia is a known risk factor, such as aviation, deep-sea diving, or space missions. While this study did not directly investigate the physiological mechanisms underlying hypoxia-induced changes, our results indicate that SIS can capture spectral disruptions reflective of cognitive degradation under oxygen-deprived states. This represents a promising direction for future work focused on the applied utility of SIS in safety-critical environments.</p>
<p id="P73">Through this work, we identify three key factors for decoding cognitive state from EEG-derived global field potentials: (A) fine-grained temporal resolution (on the order of tens of milliseconds), (B) accurate estimation of spectral intensity across physiologically meaningful frequency bands, and (C) characterization of how these spectral components interact through evolving hierarchical arrangements. We consider these to be foundational dimensions, but additional features, such as transient waveform patterns and spatial source localization, may further enhance interpretability and performance. This study contributes to the broader discussion of EEG feature selection by proposing metrics designed to capture the dynamic organization of neural activity. By examining interactions across frequency bands and tracking the temporal reorganization of oscillatory patterns in response to task demands, these features offer a more precise and responsive framework for cognitive state classification.</p></section><section id="S27"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">7. Conclusion</h2>
<p id="P74">This study establishes the concept of Granular Analysis Informing Neural Stability (GAINS), demonstrating that granular temporal interactions and hierarchical spectral intensity dynamics provide critical insights into neuronal firing patterns and their relationship to cognitive state and impairment. The Spectral Intensity Stability (SIS) algorithm outperformed others, showcasing the importance of combining spectral intensity and dynamic granular hierarchical arrangements for accurately decoding cognitive fingerprints and impairment, such as those induced by hypoxia. These three key factors – granular timescale resolution, spectral intensity, and hierarchical spectral dynamics – were identified as foundational for characterizing the neuronal criticality from these processes. While the approach is promising, further refinement and incorporation of transient behaviors and spatial dynamics are necessary for a more comprehensive understanding of neural self-organization and firing.</p></section><section id="S29" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Funding</h2>
